event: response.output_text.done
data: {"type":"response.output_text.done","sequence_number":1656,"item_id":"msg_04bf603609d0967d016b08d183310c87d29ece49e524efa368","output_index":0,"content_index":0,"text":"## Biggest technical debt: the response lifecycle is represented by loosely coupled fields\n\nMy assessment is that the highest-leverage debt is **how the application represents and coordinates an active conversation turn**. The behavior is spread across `Application`, `Session`, `chats`, and two event loops, with several fields whose values must stay synchronized by convention.\n\nThis is a judgment from reading the code, not a claim based on production incident data. Rendering performance is another plausible priority: `src/interface.rs` formats the full conversation on each draw. I would benchmark that separately, but I would address lifecycle correctness first because it governs requests, tool execution, cancellation, and recovery in both interactive and single-prompt modes.\n\n### Evidence\n\n- `src/application.rs` stores `active_request`, `response_started`, and `pending_tool_calls` independently. `src/session.rs` separately stores the turn identifier and the starting position of the current turn’s messages.\n- `src/chats.rs` coordinates these fields through imperative transitions. For example, aborting must abort the task, advance the turn identifier, clear queued calls, clear the start time, and rewrite the partial response.\n- `src/events.rs` uses `response_started.is_some()` as an indication that a response is active. The single-prompt loop additionally assumes an active task exists, waits for it, drains events, and panics if the expected completion transition did not occur.\n- The interactive loop consumes events as they arrive, while single-prompt mode waits for a task to finish before draining its events. These are different orchestration paths for the same conversation behavior.\n- Cancellation and failure remove messages from `turn_start` onward. That preserves the documented treatment of partial responses, but it also means the code must carefully distinguish conversation history from real-world tool side effects. An edit or command may already have changed something when a later step is aborted or fails.\n\nThe consequence is not that every combination currently causes a bug. The debt is that the validity of a state is difficult to establish locally, and future changes must preserve invariants across several modules. The single-prompt loop’s explicit invariant panics are a useful signal of that complexity.\n\n## Plan\n\n### 1. Establish the behavioral baseline\n\nBefore changing the implementation:\n\n1. Map the existing documented scenarios to their feature tests, especially:\n   - streaming and aborting a model response;\n   - aborting a tool call or running command;\n   - a failed request followed by another message;\n   - multiple tool calls and their results;\n   - context switching during a response;\n   - single-prompt execution, including tool use and errors.\n2. Identify gaps in externally observable behavior. Add requirements and user scenarios **before** implementing any newly agreed behavior, then add one feature test per scenario, named after the scenario.\n3. Use recorded real-provider responses for provider mocks, as required by `doc/boundaries.md`. Keep tests at the feature level.\n4. Run the existing checks and record the baseline. Preserve unrelated working-tree changes.\n\nA particularly important product decision is recovery after a tool has already caused a side effect and the turn is subsequently cancelled or fails. The current code discards the turn’s model and tool messages, but cannot generally undo a file edit or command. Decide and document what the user and subsequent model request should observe before changing that behavior.\n\n### 2. Define the lifecycle and its invariants\n\nDescribe the transition model in implementation terms, without placing implementation details in requirements documentation.\n\nA practical starting point is:\n\n| State | Meaning | Expected transition |\n|---|---|---|\n| Idle | No conversation turn is running | Start a turn |\n| Receiving response | A provider request is streaming | Complete, fail, or abort |\n| Running tool | A tool call is executing | Complete, fail, or abort |\n| Finished | The turn has reached a final response | Return to idle |\n| Failed or aborted | The turn has ended early | Return to idle after recording the appropriate outcome |\n\nRefine this model against actual behavior before coding. In particular, decide whether finished, failed, and aborted need to be stored states or are transitions back to idle.\n\nMake the invariants explicit in the design:\n\n- A running turn has one current stage and one associated background task.\n- A tool queue exists only while processing a turn that has tool calls.\n- Events are applied only to the turn and stage that produced them.\n- Completion, failure, and cancellation each leave the application ready for the next user action.\n- Conversation records and external tool effects are treated as distinct facts.\n- Statistics and elapsed time retain their existing user-visible meaning.\n\n### 3. Consolidate ownership of turn state\n\nRefactor in small steps, keeping feature tests passing:\n\n1. Introduce a cohesive turn lifecycle type owned by the application or session. Put the active stage, task handle, timing, identifier, and pending calls under that ownership where appropriate.\n2. Replace independent field checks with operations that express transitions, such as starting a response, starting a tool, completing a stage, failing a turn, and aborting a turn.\n3. Keep conversation messages in their existing domain structure initially. Avoid combining a lifecycle refactor with a provider-protocol rewrite.\n4. Make task ownership and cancellation explicit, including cleanup when the application exits or switches context.\n5. Retain event identifiers for stale-event protection. Consider a stage identifier as well as a turn identifier if the revised design permits events from successive tasks in the same turn to overlap.\n6. Remove redundant fields only after their responsibilities have moved and all callers use the new transitions.\n\nThe objective is to make invalid combinations harder to represent, not to add a large framework around a small application. The project’s architecture calls for economy of complexity.\n\n### 4. Unify event processing\n\nMake interactive and single-prompt execution use the same lifecycle transitions and completion rules.\n\n- Continue to let the event loop own conversation state.\n- Consume streamed provider and tool events through a common path.\n- Ensure task termination is observed as well as normal completion events, so an unexpectedly terminated task becomes an actionable application error rather than leaving a turn stuck or relying on a lifecycle panic.\n- Preserve interactive responsiveness, streamed display, terminal restoration, and single-prompt output and exit behavior.\n- Keep login orchestration separate unless there is a clear reason to include it; it uses the task field but has different behavior from a conversation turn.\n\nThis step should follow the state consolidation, rather than being attempted simultaneously with it.\n\n### 5. Resolve cancellation and side-effect recovery\n\nAfter agreeing on the desired behavior in step 1:\n\n1. Cover cancellation while receiving model output, while a tool is running, and after a tool has completed but before the final model response.\n2. Cover request failure after a completed tool call.\n3. Ensure the next user message produces a coherent provider conversation under the agreed policy.\n4. For running commands, verify the documented behavior that aborting stops the command.\n5. Preserve the existing display of partial output and the abort or failure indication unless requirements are deliberately changed.\n\nDo not promise that arbitrary external side effects can be rolled back. The plan should make their treatment explicit and test the behavior the application actually provides.\n\n### 6. Verify and finish\n\n- Run the project’s CI command, `nix develop --command ci`, along with focused feature tests during development.\n- Check both providers using recorded responses and cover both interactive and single-prompt paths.\n- Review error handling for terminated tasks and terminal restoration.\n- Run formatting and clippy through the project checks; do not introduce `#[allow]` attributes to bypass warnings.\n- Update `doc/architecture.md` if the event-flow description changes. Keep requirements focused on user-visible behavior.\n- Review the final change for unnecessary abstractions and confirm unrelated working-tree changes remain untouched.\n\n## Definition of done\n\nThe work is complete when existing documented behavior passes, newly agreed recovery scenarios have corresponding feature tests, and the two execution modes share a clearly owned turn lifecycle. A maintainer should be able to identify the current turn’s stage, task, cancellation behavior, and next valid transition without reconstructing relationships among independent fields across several modules.","logprobs":[]}
